Independent BFS generation, DFS/set replay and candidate-relation test oracles; full native/JS agreement on 1,154 reports with 8,461 rounds, 4,014 removed pairs and 8,166 response records. Optional unmodified mCRL2 ltscompare 202507.0 agrees on 350 systems/5,652 comparisons. All 23 native tests pass on 3.10–3.13, all 9 Node/Chrome tests pass, and an installed wheel reproduces 6 base/7 trace/10 silent controls outside the checkout through four entrypoints, checks eight module origins and rejects 20 forged reports. Actual Chrome checks 30 silent reports at 320/390/1280, 294 rounds, 498 removed-pair selections, 960 attacks, 414 response records and 10 independently verified downloads, plus 18-loop graph geometry. These are bounded coverage counts, not a proof or speed benchmark.


#### BisimLab: the loop must stay inside

[Live divergence inspector](https://midya8.github.io/bisimlab/divergence.html) · [Source and exact contract](https://github.com/midya8/bisimlab/blob/main/DIVERGENCE.md) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/bisimlab/pull/4)

Two protocol states can both loop forever and still disagree about where that loop is possible. The explicit-divergence inspector compares ordinary branching equivalence with unrooted divergence-preserving branching equivalence, enumerating every set partition of a supplied finite protocol and retaining every state's internal reachability, complete exit signatures, canonical nonempty-cycle lassos and every failed same-block obligation, then checking the coarsest valid partition explicitly. The opening six-state example reconstructs and credits Luttik2020 Example3/Figure1: both states are globally divergent, yet only one can diverge while staying in the class where the visible action remains available. A global cycle flag would miss the distinction.

Independent checks include a simple-path/block-insertion verifier without generator imports, a direct relational attack/response oracle with matrix cycle closure, 220 individually rejected non-input leaf mutations, and 1,157 complete Python/JavaScript atlases containing 8,720 partitions, 37,743 state records and 19,346 failure records. Actual unmodified pinned mCRL2 agrees on 353 frameworks / 3,672 unordered-pair-semantics comparisons. All 32 Python tests and 12 Node/Chrome tests pass; a fresh isolated wheel retains all prior controls/entrypoints, reproduces 13 new controls through both new entrypoints, checks three new module origins and rejects 26 forged reports. Actual Chrome checks 39 control-width reports at 1280/390/320, all 843 candidate selections, 4,467 state rows, 2,955 failure rows and 13 trusted completed JSON downloads verified by the native checker, with 47 complete unmasked visual states matching the reviewed implementation after deployment. Bounds: 1..6 states, 0..3 labels and 18 unique edges, at most 203 partitions; exact bounded educational model checking, not a scalable verification engine, learned model, benchmark, formal proof or agent safety/liveness guarantee.


### ShieldLab: a safe next step can still lose

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/shieldlab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab) · [Method and sources](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab/blob/main/METHOD.md)

Exact tiny safety-game inspector for hand-written agent protocols: every synchronous fixed-point obligation, winning controller move, losing rank and branching invariant/counterstrategy graph is visible. Changing only who controls a state flips the result — a one-step-safe action need not preserve safety forever — so the inspector exposes both quantifiers and every round rather than presenting a single attractive trajectory. Winning proof graphs fix a controller policy and retain every environment move; losing proof graphs fix a rank-decreasing environment counterpolicy and retain every controller move, stopping at unsafe states. A five-step forced chain, safe environment cycle, indefinite waiting and an already-unsafe root make the boundaries explicit.

Eight native tests include all 21,952 three-state graph/ownership/safety combinations and 150 seeded larger arenas. Independent bitmask Python and explicit-set JavaScript, plus a separate dual-attractor and exhaustive positional-policy checker. Three Node/actual-Chrome tests compare 643 complete reports and all six controls at 320/390/1280. Installed-wheel checks replay both CLI entry points outside the checkout. Finite total perfect-information model only: no runtime shield, LLM guarantee, liveness, timing, probability, new algorithm or formal proof; dead ends are rejected, and safe waiting need not mean progress. Established safety-game theory is attributed in the method; tests are evidence rather than proof.

ShieldLab 0.2 separates safe waiting from forced arrival. The finite reach-before-unsafe inspector exposes every synchronous attractor obligation, the exact minimum worst-case first-goal bound and a complete ranked strategy or target-avoiding counterstrategy: a winning successor can still stall, while a slower fixed policy can still win. All 24 native tests pass on Python 3.10-3.13, preserving all 8 original tests; independent strategy-pair simulation checks 324 two-state and 32,928 three-state cases, and six Node/Chrome tests compare 457 complete native/JavaScript reports, 1,329 policies and 6,291 root outcomes. Actual Chrome drills every policy/root across 12 controls at 320/390/1280. Terminal safe goals stop the play on first hit; empty targets lose; no fairness assumption forces the environment to cooperate. [Live first-goal inspector](https://midya8.github.io/shieldlab/reachavoid.html) · [Contract and eight-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab/blob/main/REACHAVOID.md).

ShieldLab 0.3 asks whether a target is visited again, not just once. The separate safe-recurrence inspector checks that a supplied finite game stays safe forever and visits a target infinitely often against every environment choice, with targets live rather than terminal. Every nested greatest/least fixed-point round and successor obligation is exposed, with both players' complete positional-policy atlases and canonical labeled unsafe or nonempty-cycle failure witnesses; one favorable path or target-containing SCC is not a recurrence guarantee. Reproduced from the exact public source: 39 Python tests, all 74,088 three-state graph/owner/safety-target configurations, 498 complete native/JavaScript/checker reports and 163 individually rejected primitive-leaf mutations; actual Chrome at 1280/390/320 checks 36 complete control reports and both policy atlases. Bounds: six states, three moves per state and at most 729 positional assignments per player. [Inspect safe recurrence](https://midya8.github.io/shieldlab/recurrence.html) · [Exact method and inspected sources](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab/blob/main/RECURRENCE.md).

Case study: [shieldlab.md](shieldlab.md)


### AbsorbLab: inspect the chain, not only the answer

[First hit](https://midya8.github.io/absorblab/) · [Strong lumping](https://midya8.github.io/absorblab/lumping.html) · [Row response](https://midya8.github.io/absorblab/response.html) · [Source and reproduction](https://github.com/midya8/absorblab)

The row-response control keeps eventual success at one while a goal edge remains positive, then drops to zero when the last exit disappears. The inspector separates the interior limit from each endpoint's actual support and direct solve. A second control has a curved rational response, and success-conditioned traffic counts visits and directed edges.

The first-hit inspector makes global path selection visible: equal direct/risky branches become 4/5 and 1/5 given eventual success, which local failure-edge removal does not explain. The strong-lumping inspector shows why equal immediate goal mass can hide a later split, and checks every partition and quotient obligation against the original transition matrix.

The current suite has 42 Python tests and 11 Node tests, including actual Chrome for all three inspectors. Full native/browser comparisons cover 213 first-hit, 527 lumping and 146 response reports; each workflow checks twelve trusted completed downloads by native replay. Installed wheels test all three workflows outside the checkout. Optional SymPy references check different numerical or algebraic claims; their support and independence limits are spelled out in the case study. These are finite descriptive checks, not performance benchmarks or proofs.

The chains are supplied, bounded to six states and use exact rational arithmetic. Conditioning is selection, not causal intervention. Strong lumping concerns the original transition matrix; response endpoints are separate chains. Undefined conditionals remain undefined, and the established math is credited rather than claimed as new theory.

Case study: [absorblab.md](absorblab.md)


### BranchLab: always possible is not inevitable

[Live workbench](https://midya8.github.io/branchlab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/branchlab) · [Method and proof limits](https://github.com/midya8/branchlab/blob/main/METHOD.md) · [Live fairness inspector](https://midya8.github.io/branchlab/fair.html)

Explicit finite CTL inspector for branching-time reasoning about supplied models: in the default, AG EF p holds while AF p fails — a goal remains reachable everywhere, but an infinite wait is still allowed. Twelve original controls separate nested path quantifiers, different existential witnesses, strong-until failure, time-zero success and finite versus lasso counterexamples, and no fairness assumption silently removes an inconvenient branch. Python and browser JavaScript expose every unique subformula's truth set, synchronous least/greatest rounds, all-state obligations and the complete 2^n subset-image atlas; a separate native checker derives truth by enumerating all fixed points and independently enumerates finite prefixes and first-repeat lassos.

Fifteen native tests run on Python 3.10–3.13 with four outside-checkout installed-wheel checks; all 144 two-state graph/labelling cases pass and a direct path/lasso oracle covers 2,744 three-state cases. Genuine pyModelChecking 1.3.4 agreement covers 212 requests and 3,630 subformula truth sets, and complete browser/native records agree on 276 requests; actual Chrome checks 36 control/width reports, 192 subformulas, 558 state selections, 237 rounds, 1,110 subset rows and 12 completed exports replayed natively. Classical bounded CTL, not symbolic scale, LTL/CTL*, verified AI agents or new theory; runtime needs no paid API or external package, and the optional GPL test reference is not vendored.

BranchLab 0.2 asks which infinite paths an assumption really leaves. The separate state-justice fair-CTL inspector compares ordinary and fair truth at every subformula, exposes every induced region and its recurrent components, and shows all justice hits, backward rounds and a replayable finite-prefix/closed-walk witness. Its original four-state hub needs the repeated walk s0 → s1 → s0 → s2 → s0 to meet both justice constraints; neither first simple cycle is enough. A trap can still satisfy a local atom while having no fair infinite continuation. Twenty-nine Python tests and seven Node tests pass, retaining the original ordinary-CTL workflows. Native Tarjan generation, an independent Boolean-closure/recurrent-subset checker and a separately written browser Kosaraju engine agree on 278 full reports, 2,808 region records, 4,135 subformulas and 2,643 fair lassos; 519 changed noninput primitive leaves are rejected. Actual NetworkX 3.4.2 checks all regions, 4,632 components and all lassos as a graph reference, not a full fair-CTL oracle. The existing actual pyModelChecking 1.3.4 ordinary reference still matches 212 requests and 3,630 subformula sets; its fairness route is explicitly excluded after three reproducible disagreements. Real Chrome exercises 36 fair reports at 1280/390/320 pixels, 216 regions, 612 region/start selections, 465 subformula selections, 390 witness rows and 2,628 graph edges, with twelve trusted downloads independently replayed. Fresh installed wheels confirm nine module origins, twelve fair controls through both entrypoints, 24 forged CLI atomic-preservation checks and 24 direct forgeries, retaining ordinary installation checks. All 46 exact public files, feature/PR and main CI, Pages, deployed assets, behavior and 43 unmasked screenshot comparisons were verified. Bounded explicit checking for supplied total graphs of at most six states and four state-based justice sets; every constraint must recur infinitely often on the same path. Fair EG searches inside the operand region before checking recurrence; intersecting ordinary EG with globally fair states is not sound. An empty justice set excludes every path, while no justice constraints recovers ordinary CTL. These are generalized-Büchi execution assumptions, not strong/compassion fairness, an implemented scheduler, new model-checking theory, a scalable symbolic solver or a guarantee about real AI agents. [Fairness method and checks](https://github.com/midya8/branchlab/blob/main/FAIRNESS.md) · [Pull request 2](https://github.com/midya8/branchlab/pull/2).

Case study: [branchlab.md](branchlab.md)


### ProvLab: one fact is not two chances
 · [Live chase workbench](https://midya8.github.io/provlab/chase.html)
[Live workbench](https://midya8.github.io/provlab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/provlab) · [Method and sources](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/blob/main/METHOD.md) · [Implementation PR](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/pull/1) · [Live containment lab](https://midya8.github.io/provlab/containment.html)

A finite relational-provenance workbench for symbolic data reasoning: shared query variables perform real joins, and reusing a token is one event, not a fresh independent draw. The default query derives `answer(amy)` through `R(x) ∧ R(x)` or `R(x) ∧ S(x)`, with provenance `p² + pq` — bag multiplicity `10` at `p=2, q=3`, and exact answer-presence probability `1/2` at `P(p)=1/2, P(q)=1/4`, not the ordinary substitution `3/8`, because the first route already succeeds whenever `p` exists and the second adds no new successful worlds. Fourteen original controls cover token reuse, duplicate union-all branches, overlapping events, projection collisions, join chains, repeated variables, tagged constants, empty answers, Boolean heads, bag zero, probability 0/1, triple reuse and an empty relation.

A separate native verifier imports no join generator: it enumerates active-domain valuations and re-queries possible databases, and Shannon recursion checks the marginal independently of the generator's world-mass sum. SQLite token-carrying joins feed actual SymPy 1.14.0 polynomial checks over 164 requests, 3,062 possible-database queries and 233 polynomial outputs. Twenty-one native tests run on Python 3.10–3.13, including 64 exhaustive two-value cases, 150 seeded queries and all 93 non-input primitive mutations of the default report; browser/native parity compares 164 full reports and 6,310 output/world records, and actual Chrome runs all 14 controls at 1280, 390 and 320px with 14 trusted certificate downloads. Classical provenance semirings and tuple-independent base-event semantics, not new theory, arbitrary SQL, scalable inference, calibrated probabilities or a guarantee about an AI agent.


ProvLab 0.2 compares safe positive conjunctive queries over every finite database under set semantics: a head-preserving reverse homomorphism proves containment, while a failed direction produces a concrete frozen canonical database and a missing output tuple. The inspector exposes every variable-map candidate, its atom images and ordered head constraints, both canonical evaluations, and every safe atom-subset minimum; constants stay fixed, Boolean heads and ternary relations are explicit, and repeated atom occurrences remain visible. The default repeated-fact rewrite preserves the answer set but changes a multiplicity probe from 4 to 2 and natural provenance from f0² to f0 — set-equivalent is not bag-equivalent. Thirty-two Python tests include 235 single-field corruption rejections and 80 direct finite-instance checks; 112 cross-language comparisons cover 17,403 candidate maps and 1,662 subset rows; actual SQLite DISTINCT/ALL/token joins ran 1,344 queries and SymPy checked 362 polynomials built from those token rows. Bounded single-CQ syntax under set semantics; classical Chandra-Merlin query-containment and core theory, not new theory, a bag-equivalence decision procedure, arbitrary SQL optimization, a language model, or a verified-AI-agent guarantee. [Containment method and reproduction](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/blob/main/CONTAINMENT.md) · [Pull request 2](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/pull/2).

ProvLab 0.3 adds constraint-aware containment under full single-head rules and relational keys: a finite chase makes query rewrites inspectable — full rules add facts, keys merge query symbols in both the database and the ordered output tuple — with both containment directions, every chase step, terminal dependency checks, source derivations and legal saturated counterexamples visible. A clash between distinct literals is labeled as target-query unsatisfiability and vacuous containment, not a successful nonempty rewrite. Every dependency subset is tested: the default needs a key plus a diagonal rule, neither alone suffices, and a symmetry rule is irrelevant; another control separates a one-rule minimum support from a two-rule inclusion-minimal support. Forty-four Python tests, 114 complete native/JavaScript reports, an independent ground replay, 344 primitive evidence mutations rejected, actual SQLite dependency/query checks and installed-wheel reproduction; actual Chrome checks 14 controls at 1280/390/320px, both directions, 171 trace states, 141 subset selections and 14 trusted certificate downloads replayed by Python. Bounded classical database theory, not existential chasing, arbitrary EGDs, SQL NULL, bag-preserving optimization or global query minimization; the original provenance and unconstrained-containment labs remain available. [Chase method and checks](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/blob/main/CHASE.md) · [Pull request 3](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/pull/3).

Case study: [provlab.md](provlab.md)


### BuchiLab: a winning state is not a winning policy

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/buchilab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/buchilab) · [Method and seven-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/buchilab/blob/main/METHOD.md)

Tiny finite adversarial repeated-goal games with every nested greatest/least fixed-point obligation, progress rank, complete positional controller policy and reachable cyclic non-goal SCC visible. Idle can stay inside the winning region forever and still lose: rank-guided progress and per-policy audits show why. A separate checker replays with sets/Floyd reachability and every joint-policy eventual lasso, without calling the generator. Seven original controls distinguish recurrence from one-time reachability, environment starvation from controller choice, full-graph SCCs from non-goal induced cycles, and an empty controller policy from no policy. Every fixed-point update includes unchanged final rounds. Input is bounded to six states and three moves per state. All environment moves are retained in policy audits, and full report JSON can be exported and independently checked.

Nine native tests include 21,952 exhaustive three-state games; three Node/actual-Chrome tests compare 331 complete Python/JavaScript reports and every control record at 320/390/1280. The generator uses bitmasks/Tarjan/BFS, the browser uses sets and graph-search closure, and the native checker uses sets/Floyd closure plus exact deterministic eventual-lasso replay for every joint positional-policy pair. Installed wheels replay seven original controls through both CLIs outside checkout. No fairness assumption, learned abstraction, deployed shield, general LTL compiler, formal proof or real-agent guarantee. Rank-permitted actions are sufficient, not all winning policies. Testing is not proof: supplied graphs can omit real behavior or mislabel goals. Attribution to primary papers, academic notes and official tool documentation is in the method.

Case study: [buchilab.md](buchilab.md)


#### BuchiLab: repeat the actual failure

[Action-level lasso inspector](https://midya8.github.io/buchilab/lassos.html) · [Exact witness contract](https://github.com/midya8/buchilab/blob/main/LASSOS.md)

A reachable bad component now becomes an exact action-labelled prefix once and nonempty non-goal cycle forever, for every failing fixed controller policy/root. Empty prefixes, self-loops, multiedge cycles, unreachable bad components and duplicate-target action choices are explicit. BFS generation is independently checked with bounded simple walks and the joint-policy lasso oracle. Original v1 reports and original tests stay intact.

16 native tests across four Python versions and five Node/actual-Chrome tests, including 314 complete witness-report comparisons and every record in ten controls at 320/390/1280. Installed wheels replay both report families outside checkout. Input-order anchor, shortest prefix/cycle individually, not globally shortest lasso. These are per-policy counterexamples, not one environment strategy defeating every controller policy or a real-agent guarantee.

### EUFLab: ground equality with evidence

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/euflab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/euflab) · [Method and six-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/euflab/blob/main/METHOD.md)

Proof-producing ground equality: every ordered equal pair has a proof DAG, and every non-entailed equality in a consistent input has a separating finite interpretation. Functions preserve equality, not difference — f(a)=f(b) does not imply a=b, while a=b implies f(a)=f(b) — and the inspector makes both directions visible with non-injectivity, cyclic values, duplicate syntax, ordered arguments and inconsistent-premise controls. Native union-find/proof forests and a separate JavaScript relation engine agree, with independent proof/model verification and exhaustive set-partition references.

Nine native tests include partition-enumeration references, 27 exhaustive constant conjunctions, 64 four-term assertion subsets plus all added disequality probes, 80 seeded DAGs, proof tampering and atomic CLI rejection; two Node tests compare 156 semantic reports and native-check every browser proof DAG, and Chrome tests all 251 ordered queries and every displayed proof/model row at 320/390/1280, with installed-wheel reproduction through both entry points.

The finite-model atlas enumerates every compatible term-equality pattern, finds minimum domain size and classifies each pair as forced equal, forced different or flexible — the most separated quotient is not necessarily the smallest model. It covers all 4,140 eight-name patterns with 16 native tests retaining the original nine, six Node/Chrome tests, 76 complete cross-engine atlas reports and all 4,317 admitted control patterns. The domain-cap debugger holds the domain cap fixed and enumerates every inclusion-minimal assertion conflict and deletion repair, separating intrinsic inconsistency from cap-only conflicts; it carries 23 native tests, nine Node/Chrome tests and 269 complete cross-engine reports, with Chrome auditing 379 subset selections, 36 core-relaxation actions, 29 repair actions and 104 cap changes.

The costed repair planner enumerates every permitted deletion set, every cost-only optimum and a separate cost/count Pareto frontier, with protected intrinsic or cap-only cores explaining infeasibility and each plan including a total minimum-domain model with kept, protected and omitted assertion truth rows; deleting does not mean negating — the live control shows an omitted equality that still holds. Fourteen synthetic controls cover different-size/same-cost optima, two-coordinate tradeoffs, duplicate occurrences, hard obstructions and function-driven cardinality, and native and browser engines agree on 358 complete reports. The exact repair-cost sensitivity stage varies one assertion deletion cost over every integer from 0 to 1,000,000, giving exact keep/delete intervals, breakpoint ties and every optimum mask including zero-cost nonminimal repairs, with an independent native verifier rebuilding all affine plan-cost lines. Bounded to eight terms, arity at most two, eight equalities and eight disequalities, one uninterpreted sort and ground conjunctions only: classical congruence closure, not new theory, a scalable solver or a real-code verifier.

Case study: [euflab.md](euflab.md)


### HallLab: distinct is a global promise

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/halllab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/halllab) · [Method and six-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/halllab/blob/main/METHOD.md)

Exact all-different propagation for tiny constraint-based AI assignment, where distinctness is a global promise, not a pairwise one: every retained variable/value is backed by a complete injective assignment, and every pruned edge is explained by a forced-residual Hall obstruction. Two variables reserving {v0, v1} leave seven edges under pairwise disequalities but five under global support, forcing x2 to v2; a second control has three variables sharing two values, where every binary constraint has support but no full assignment exists. Twelve native tests include 8,777 exhaustive domain graphs and 120 seeded larger inputs, with 682 full Python/JavaScript report comparisons and actual Chrome checking every control edge, subset and propagation round at 320/390/1280; CI spans Python 3.10-3.13 and an installed wheel replays the controls outside the checkout.

HallLab's overlapping-scope stage repeats full local propagation to a synchronous fixed point and compares it with exact common-assignment support: a two-color triangle keeps every local value but has no joint assignment, and a satisfiable triangle keeps local values that never occur in a common solution. Eight new native tests and three new Node/Chrome tests cover 621 overlap reports and nine browser controls at 320/390/1280, over at most six variables, eight values and six scopes (262144 full tuples). [Overlapping-scope inspector](https://midya8.github.io/halllab/overlaps.html) · [Exact overlap contract and sources](https://github.com/midya8/halllab/blob/main/OVERLAPS.md).

The conflict stage enumerates all inclusion-minimal conflicting scope sets and all inclusion-minimal scope-removal repairs, with complete single-deletion witnesses: the default diamond has a size-one and four size-two minimal removals, so minimal is not minimum. Twenty-eight native tests and nine Node/actual-Chrome tests cover 621 cross-engine reports and 4413 active subsets; domains stay fixed, and an empty-domain background has no scope-removal repair. [Live conflict inspector](https://midya8.github.io/halllab/conflicts.html) · [Exact contract and seven-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/halllab/blob/main/CONFLICTS.md).

HallLab 0.4 adds an alternating-path inspector: one variable and two values produce three singleton alternating SCCs, yet both domain edges have support — the inspector follows a free value through an even alternating path, flips its edges and shows the resulting full assignment, with dummy-node SCCs exposing the same characterization without hiding where the spare value starts. Thirty-six Python tests and 12 Node/Chrome tests cover 5,058 tiny domain graphs, 120 seeded bound-wide cases, 821 complete cross-language reports and 136 primitive forgeries, with pinned Choco 4.10.18 AC_REGIN propagation agreeing over 821 cases; the deployed inspector replays all 12 controls at 1280/390/320, every edge certificate, reach layer, SCC and flip step. At most six variables and eight values; classical bounded all-different theory, not a new algorithm or a production incremental solver. [Live alternating inspector](https://midya8.github.io/halllab/alternating.html) · [Exact contract and eight-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/halllab/blob/main/ALTERNATING.md) · [Pull request](https://github.com/midya8/halllab/pull/4).

Case study: [halllab.md](halllab.md)



### ChannelLab: same accuracy, different information

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/channellab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/channellab) · [Method and attributed theory](https://github.com/midya8/channellab/blob/main/METHOD.md)

Exact finite binary experiment comparison where one task ties and the information order does not: at a uniform prior, two channels both reach exactly 3/4 optimal classification accuracy, yet neither simulates the other — a passing direction constructs a state-independent garbling kernel, a failed direction supplies a two-action decision witness with a strict value gap. Thirteen developed controls include relabelled and split signals that stay equivalent across different output alphabets, a perfect signal that adds information without helping a task whose best action ignores the state, and a disclosed large-fraction control needing integer outputs beyond JavaScript's safe range. 30 Python tests and six Node/actual-Chrome tests cover 710 complete cross-engine reports, 6,994 task policies, 627 feasible kernels and 793 strict counterexamples, with 1,420 independent HiGHS feasibility solves agreeing on all 627 feasible cases.

Version 0.2 adds an exact all-prior decision atlas: holding the channels and payoff table fixed, every deterministic policy keeps an exact affine value and a closed optimality range over the whole prior interval [0,1], with all tied policies and every ranking root retained. For the default classification task the knots are 0, 1/3, 1/2, 2/3 and 1 — A wins below 1/2, B above, the uniform prior ties. Atlas parity covers 710 complete cases with 8,486 policies, 3,401 knots and 2,691 open intervals, and 16,972 independent HiGHS LPs rebuild every policy-optimal prior range with all 6,251 nonempty ranges agreeing. [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/channellab/pull/2).

### AuctionLab: truthful alone, not protected together

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/auctionlab/) · [Source](https://github.com/midya8/auctionlab) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/auctionlab/pull/1)

Exact finite multiagent allocation where individual honesty and coalition stability answer different questions: in the developed two-item, three-bidder default, giving A to B2 and B to B3 creates welfare 12, each winner pays the VCG externality 10 - 6 = 4, revenue is 8 and truthful utilities are [0,2,2] — yet the seller-inclusive coalition is short by 2, a cooperative-core deficit and a mathematical blocking witness, not a collusion strategy or a market recommendation. Changing only B1's row to [0,5,5,10] keeps welfare at 12, raises revenue to 10 and eliminates the deficit in that control. The report-independent bundle menu prices every bundle from the other reports alone: at the default, B3 can take bundle B for price 4 and utility 2 while bundle AB costs 10 for utility -4, and every efficient truthful allocation chooses a best menu bundle.

Every tie stays in the evidence: the tied single-good control keeps both winning allocations with payment vectors [7,0] and [0,7], utilities [0,0] and revenue 7, and the zero-report one-bidder control keeps every tie with true utility gains spanning -9 to 0. A separate certificate verifier rebuilds the whole report from disjoint bundle products without importing the generator, and strict recursive comparison rejects missing, duplicated, reordered, extra and mistyped evidence. 24 Python tests over a 52-profile corpus with 1,997 allocations, 163 efficient outcomes, 272 coalition rows and 752 menu entries; an exhaustive audit of all 196 two-bidder profiles over 14 monotone two-good value rows with 5,488 reports and 12,348 reported-optimal outcomes; 1,024 input-built HiGHS binary integer solves with 5,530 constraint rows and zero objective error at 1e-8 tolerance; actual Chrome over 48 responsive views with 1,581 allocation selections, 636 menu entries, 312 coalition rows and 3,441 deviation outcomes. Merged main retains the initial commit and feature history, all 47 public files match the checked local source, and all five deployed assets match merged source byte for byte. At most four items and three bidders with valuations 0..100, free disposal, zero seller reserve and quasilinear private values — established VCG theory, not a new theorem, a market recommendation or a real auction.

Case study: [auctionlab.md](auctionlab.md)


### MatchLab: stable is not cost-optimal

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/matchlab/) · [Source](https://github.com/midya8/matchlab) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/matchlab/pull/1)

Exact finite two-sided matching where stability and optimization answer different questions: in the developed three-by-three default, A rankings are `1>2>0`, `0>2>1`, `2>1>0` and B rankings are `2>0>1`, `1>2>0`, `1>0>2`, with supplied cost rows `(6,0,0)`, `(6,3,2)`, `(1,7,11)`. The unconstrained minimum (1,2,0) costs 3 but is blocked by (A2,B1); the cheapest stable assignment (2,0,1) costs 13, so requiring stability costs 10 — and running either side's deferred acceptance does not select it. The A-proposing extreme (1,0,2) costs 17 and the B-proposing extreme (0,2,1) costs 15.

20 Python tests over 76 complete reports: 2,219 assignments, 49,295 pair witnesses, 113 stable assignments, 217 lattice operations and 715 proposal states; 1,372 changed derived primitives rejected across three controls; full 76-report Python/JavaScript parity; 152 input-built HiGHS solves with 1,774 constraint rows and zero objective error at 1e-8 tolerance; actual Chrome over 48 responsive views with 1,149 selected assignments and 23,793 pair rows; 16 exact reports downloaded and independently replayed. A four-by-four control shows the stable family need not be a chain: four stable assignments form a diamond lattice, with matchings 1 and 6 incomparable and A-oriented join 0 and meet 7. At most five per side, strict complete rankings, signed integer costs — classical Gale-Shapley deferred acceptance, not a new algorithm or a fairness claim.

Case study: [matchlab.md](matchlab.md)


### ParetoLab: nondominated is not supported

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/paretolab/) · [Source](https://github.com/midya8/paretolab) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/paretolab/pull/1)

Exact finite biobjective DAG planning where nondominance and weighted-sum selection answer different questions: in the developed default, three parallel paths (0,12), (7,7) and (12,0) are all nondominated, yet the middle one needs t >= 7/12 and t <= 5/12 to minimize t*cost1 + (1-t)*cost2 — the intersection is empty, so no positive weighted sum selects it. A hard second-cost ceiling of 7 makes (7,7) the lexicographic optimum instead, and a 7/12–5/12 whole-path lottery over the extremes yields expected costs (5,7) while the first extreme violates the realized ceiling with probability 7/12.

Every weight comparison is an exact linear inequality in t, with reduced rational half-lines intersected with [0,1] defining point cells and open intervals of constant optimizer identity; the reverse-node dynamic program prunes strictly dominated suffixes while the complete full-path census stays separate. 20 Python tests; a 729-profile exhaustive audit; 52 complete native/browser parity reports with 101 paths, 176 weight cells and 130 thresholds; 556 input-built HiGHS solves with 2,403 rows and maximum objective error 8.9e-16 at 1e-8 tolerance; actual Chrome over 48 responsive views with 220 path selections, 208 weight cells, 180 budget states and 324 label rows. Merged main retains the initial commit and feature history, all 31 public files match the checked local source, and all five deployed assets are byte-exact. Scope is 2..7 nodes, at most 12 arcs, two additive integer costs 0..20 — supplied costs, not learned preferences, and a classical phenomenon illustrated with an original teaching control, not a new theorem.

Case study: [paretolab.md](paretolab.md)

### RiskLab: the plan you would change

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/risklab/) · [Source](https://github.com/midya8/risklab) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/risklab/pull/1)

Exact finite scenario-tree decisions under static and nested CVaR, where the same risk measure evaluated once at the root and re-evaluated at a reached decision can disagree: in the developed `reversal` control at confidence alpha 1/4, the unique static optimum p3 (expected loss 1, static CVaR 7/3) is not the nested optimum p2 (nested CVaR 22/9), and at the second reached decision p3's conditional static CVaR is 10 against a best continuation of 28/3. In the `ignored_branch` control both policies share nested root value 10 though one chooses loss 2 where loss 1 is available — only backward Bellman selection picks the locally optimal continuation.

16 Python tests; a separate tail-fill checker rejecting 1,608 altered primitive leaves; 821 complete Python/checker/BigInt report comparisons over twelve controls, 80 seeded trees and all 729 grid combinations with 1,121 policies; 9,896 HiGHS LP solves with worst error below 1.8e-15 at 1e-8 tolerance; actual Chrome over 36 control/width reports with 216 policy selections, 1,518 leaf rows, 2,334 node rows, 696 continuation rows and 120 nested chance proofs. Scope is at most 15 nodes, eight leaves, four decisions, depth six and 64 policies — supplied teaching trees, not a learned agent, financial advice or a real-world safety guarantee.

Case study: [risklab.md](risklab.md)

### RectLab: a cover is not a partition

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/rectlab/) · [Source](https://github.com/midya8/rectlab) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/rectlab/pull/1)

Exact finite Boolean factorization where a cover, a partition and a lower bound answer different questions: in the developed default, the 4×4 matrix `0101 / 1101 / 1110 / 1011` has 11 positive cells and 41 nonempty all-one rectangles; every cover needs at least four rectangles, but the maximum fooling set is only three, so the largest fooling set does not certify the minimum cover. The unrestricted optimum has 68 unordered minimum covers; restricting factors to maximal rectangles leaves the size at four and reduces the count to 13. Version 0.2 adds random access by canonical zero-based decimal index: R0 appears in exactly one of the 68, R12 in 40, and under the maximal-only restriction R12's count is nine out of thirteen while R0 is ineligible — and in the unique-cover overlap control all three selected factors are mandatory.

18 Python tests; an exhaustive class of all 512 binary 3×3 matrices; 64 complete reports over nine controls and 55 seeded inputs; 159 input-built HiGHS models over 53 nonempty inputs with exact objectives; actual Chrome over 36 control-width views with 108 witness-family selections, 520 optimal-set inspections, 2,988 membership rows and 192 maximum-fooling selections. The overlap control `1011 / 1100 / 1110 / 0001` has a unique minimum cover of three rectangles while every disjoint partition needs four. Scope is at most five rows, five columns and twelve positive cells — a bounded exact tool, not a new ranking algorithm or a statistical sampler.

Case study: [rectlab.md](rectlab.md)

### GraphLab: a pair can flip what singles cannot

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/graphlab/) · [Source](https://github.com/midya8/graphlab) · [Joint-deletion contract](https://github.com/midya8/graphlab/blob/main/JOINT.md)

Joint deletion is a complete census, not an additive guess: in the hand-set counterexample — four scalar features [-4,-4,6,6], five original edges, baseline margin 0.5534180126 — all five single deletions preserve class 1, yet removing e1=(0,2) and e3=(1,2) together produces margin -0.6666666667 and class 0, while singleton extrapolation predicts +0.3151513784, a residual of -0.9818180451. Of all 16 empty/single/pair sets, five change the class, and the stronger minimum-margin pair e1/e2 has margin -1.0606601718 and remains visible.

51 Python tests retain all 42 original tests; independent dense/bitmask checks cover 180 seeded graph/model cases, 70-digit Decimal checks cover all 16 counterexample sets, Python/JavaScript agree on 46 complete reports and 559 subsets, and actual NumPy checks all 559 logit vectors with max difference 8.88e-16. Five Node/Chrome tests cover 24 responsive views, 568 subset selections and six natively replayed trusted downloads. Preflight sums binomial coefficients through min(k,m) with native limits k=0..8 and 1..65,536 forwards; the original 483-edge graph at k=2 exceeds the cap while k=1 fits. Scope is a bounded binary64 fixed-model sensitivity census over original edges only — not a causal effect, learned robustness improvement or new algorithm.

Case study: [graphlab.md](graphlab.md)



### QuantLab: zero weight error is not a preserved decision

[Live activation audit](https://midya8.github.io/quantlab/integer.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/quantlab) - [Method and seven-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/quantlab/blob/main/INTEGER.md)

QuantLab 0.3 traces activation x weight integer dot products, not only packed weights. A fixed user-declared activation range produces signed 8-bit codes; every integer product and partial accumulator stays visible, and exact rational witnesses separate weight error from activation error. In the constructed default (W=[[127,0],[0,127]], x=[0.4,0.49], activation range 127, stored scales exactly 1) the exact-real scores are 127 times the represented inputs with row 1 strictly larger, yet the quantized activations are [0,0], so every product and accumulator is 0 and row 0 wins the tie. Weight error is exactly 0; activation error explains the whole change. The weight-only audit was not wrong, it was incomplete: it measured the weights and never touched the activations, so a zero weight error looked like a preserved decision.

47 Python tests retain all 39 earlier tests; independent floor/remainder rounding checks 240 seeded reports and all 519 signed half-unit controls; Python Fraction and independent browser BigInt dyadics agree on 72 complete reports with 6,848 products and all exact error strings; actual NumPy 2.2.6 int32 matmul and cumsum check every accumulator and prefix. Seven Node/actual-Chrome tests cover 168 row selections, 20 responsive views, two trusted downloads replayed natively and stale-result rejection. A fresh isolated eight-module package reproduces original training, all 52 old reports and all 72 new reports. Scope is a constructed arithmetic control, not trained performance: integer products and prefixes are exact under the checked shape bound of 2,064,512, final output rescaling uses binary64, and the activation range is supplied, not calibrated.

Case study: [quantlab.md](quantlab.md)

### EmbedKit: twenty retries are not unlimited rejection

[Live sampler audit](https://midya8.github.io/embedkit/sampler.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/embedkit) - [Math and eight-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/embedkit/blob/main/SAMPLER.md)

EmbedKit now audits the noise behind its negatives. The unchanged sampler excludes positive context and earlier negatives, retries 20 times, then returns the first eligible sorted word — and that fallback is part of the law, not an implementation detail. Native Fraction and independent browser BigInt count exact 53-bit proposal intervals and propagate every reachable bounded subset state, comparing finite retry against unlimited conditional rejection on the same proposals. In the constructed default (counts a:1, b:1, z:1,000,000, positive context z excluded) the fallback probability is 0.9987359285, selection of sorted-first a is 0.9993679643 and of b is 0.0006320357 — while unlimited rejection on the same stored-threshold proposal gives about 0.5 each. The tiny a/b asymmetry comes from exact grid endpoints, not an imposed equality. The geometric factor G — the sum of e^t for t=0..A-1, where e is the excluded proposal mass — names the mechanism: each eligible word receives its proposal mass times G, and the sorted first eligible word additionally receives e^A. A sampler that falls back after twenty failures is a different distribution from one that rejects until it succeeds; calling the first rejection sampling misstates what it returns.

61 Python tests retain all 53 prior tests and 40 original trainer tests; eight Node/actual-Chrome tests retain all 4 prior tests. 33 complete reports agree on 2,085 exact rational strings and 3,682 numeric values, with maximum normalized decimal-display delta 2.22e-16. Independent enumeration checks 10,844 small-grid random tuples and 289 unlimited-conditional ordered paths; scripted calls exercise 1,659 actual original-sampler grid boundaries plus success/retry/fallback consumption. All 195 witness states, 20 responsive views and 2 trusted downloads are checked, and a fresh 10-module installed package reproduces original training, all 19 old step reports and all 33 sampler reports through both new entrypoints. Scope is a constructed audit, not a training claim: exact probabilities assume independent uniform-grid proposals, multi-draw audits are bounded to 8 words, the 32-word synthetic corpus is one draw only, and at e=1 finite retry returns after A draws while unlimited rejection never returns, so the comparator is undefined, not uniform.

Case study: [embedkit.md](embedkit.md)

### RAGLine: a zero can win

[Live engine inspector](https://midya8.github.io/ragline/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/ragline) - [Contract, limits and credited sources](https://github.com/midya8/ragline/blob/main/POSTINGS.md)

RAGLine’s retrieval loop now has a second backend: an opt-in fixed-corpus signed-vector postings index that runs the actual retrieval, with the original scan retained as the default and extraction, citations and lexical grounding unchanged. The surprise is what an implicit zero does — a row never visited by a posting contributes zero, and that zero can outrank a negative match, while shared-bin products can cancel to zero — so the sparse representation has its own arithmetic, and the ranking it returns is not the dense ranking with terms skipped. RAGLine keeps the complete raw-score stable ranking intact before slicing and six-decimal score display, so the order shown is the order the arithmetic produced.

49 Python tests retain all 31 originals byte-for-byte. Independent dense references cover 729 exhaustive signed-cube controls, 400 seeded real-vector matrices and 1,350 actual hashed-pipeline query/k comparisons. Four Node/actual-Chrome tests check 24 complete integer-engine reports plus 200 seeded native/browser reports, all 186 rows, 1,161 products and 186 signed SVG marks, stale/invalid state, 320/390/1280 containment and two trusted exports replayed natively. A separate installed wheel checks nine module origins, API/module/console reports, 45 real-pipeline fixture comparisons and both CLI gate sides. A constructed 32×64 control visits one posting versus 2,048 dense dot-coordinate products, but the query still makes all row scores and fully sorts the corpus. Construction retains a dense snapshot plus postings and cached norms; rebuild after corpus/fit changes. Signed collisions, extractive copying and lexical grounding retain their original limits. No new method or retrieval-quality claim.

### PlanBench: the cheap run that failed

[Live retry-risk inspector](https://midya8.github.io/planbench/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/planbench) - [Exact model and sources](https://github.com/midya8/planbench/blob/main/RISK.md)

PlanBench now computes the exact finite retry-risk law for a supplied agent DAG: the joint plan-success, completion-time and cost law, including exhausted retries, skip cascades and shared ancestors — a shared ancestor is sampled once, not separately per path. The surprise is what a failure does to a budget: a cheap failed run can satisfy a budget by skipping the work, so within-limit probability is not successful-completion probability. A half-failing prerequisite followed by a five-time-unit dependent step has probability 1/2 of fitting time1/cost1 but zero probability of succeeding within those limits, and for two independent half-failing parallel steps with one retry each the expected makespan is 7/4 rather than the maximum individual expected duration 3/2. Native code branches only on executed terminal outcomes, while the independent browser enumerates all potential terminal assignments, schedules completion events and sums unused skipped-step outcomes.

39 Python tests retain all 29 originals. Four Node/actual-Chrome tests compare 28 complete Fraction/BigInt reports, 260 terminal histories, 177 joint atoms, 778 selected step rows, 24 responsive views and native-replayed trusted exports. An independent Bernoulli-bit/completion-event oracle checks 65 seeded DAGs and retry boundaries, and an installed wheel reproduces both new CLI entrypoints and the original validate/simulate gates outside the checkout. Complete histories retain final-attempt starts, dependency-ready times, costs and unconditional/conditional summaries; zero-success conditional expectations stay undefined. At most six steps and two retries each; failure rates, fixed durations and costs are declared, not learned, and calls fail independently with unlimited workers and immediate retries, excluding resource contention, backoff, timeouts and correlated outages. Exact rational laws are not production reliability, empirical confidence or a new scheduling algorithm, and the original seeded simulator stays unchanged.

### DistillLab: the same target is not the same gradient

[Target/rest workbench](https://midya8.github.io/distilllab/tail.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/distilllab) - [Equations and replay contract](https://github.com/midya8/distilllab/blob/main/TAIL.md)

DistillLab's v0.3 workbench audits the established KL chain rule: binary target/rest KL plus teacher rest mass times conditional-tail KL. Four one-step variants — the original tail, a uniform tail, a one-class cyclic rotation and a configured rotation/uniform mix — all keep the supplied-label target/rest mass at the selected temperature and all begin from the same original student, yet they produce different student gradients. The target is the supplied training label, not necessarily the teacher's argmax, and a rotation preserves entropy in exact arithmetic without preserving class meaning or temperature-1 confidence — so the target probability alone does not determine the update; the shape of the rest does. Python computes analytic MLP backprop while the browser independently differentiates scalar tanh/logits/subset-logsumexp operations through reverse-mode automatic differentiation, exposing every class derivative, hard/binary/coupled-tail parameter component, full-class cross-check, simultaneous update and raw residual. CE and KL differ by a fixed-teacher entropy offset that can change between tail variants, and true excluded-class normalization avoids both a finite minus-1000 mask and subtracting a rounded target probability from one.

36 Python tests retain all 24 earlier tests. Seven substantive Node/Chrome tests (eight runner entries) check 28 full witnesses, 112 variants, 21,724 parameter positions and 286,603 cross-runtime numeric fields, with 2,368 centered finite-difference component checks, 40 80-digit Decimal controls, all seven archived states across all 80 training rows and 336 separately altered report leaves. Actual Chrome checks 84 input/width layouts, 24,012 class cells, 521,376 parameter cells, 65,172 coordinate selections and six completed downloads, with three downloaded archives replayed natively. Installed wheels outside checkout check nine module origins, exact witness regeneration, both new CLI entrypoints, 56 valid archive replays and 56 rejected forgeries; bounded input and full report/replay schemas reject unknown fields, duplicate keys, nonfinite values and malformed shapes. The decomposition is established work attributed to Zhao et al. (CVPR 2022) and standard KL algebra, not a new method — no DKD training reproduction, held-out evidence, causal account of the original accuracy gain, trained arm, test-label gradient or selected hyperparameter — and all original trainer/model/data/report results and the earlier inspector remain unchanged.

### VisionLab: an attack is not a certificate

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/ai-lab/tree/main/visionlab)

VisionLab's interval-bound-propagation work makes the distinction concrete: an attack upper-bounds robust accuracy and a certificate lower-bounds it, and the two can disagree by almost everything. The FGSM-trained model keeps 86% of test images under a 20-step PGD attack at eps = 0.03, yet interval bounds certify 0.037 at eps = 0.01 and 0.000 at 0.03 - the bounds are loose for a network never trained to be tight, so "no certificate" does not mean "attackable". Training through the bounds (loss = 0.5 clean CE + 0.5 worst-case CE, eps and the mix ramped over 40% of the steps) proves 56.1% of test images safe at eps = 0.03 and 30.5% at 0.05, with median certified radius 0.0363 against 0.0011 for the standard model - at a price of about 15 points of clean accuracy (0.839 vs 0.988), and its PGD accuracy at 0.03 (0.718) is below the adversarially trained model's (0.859): certifiability was bought with raw accuracy, not for free.

66 tests pin the machinery behind the claim: gradient checks (float64 finite differences) for every layer and the whole network, exactness of the bounds at eps = 0 and on a single linear layer, certified radii bracketing the certification boundary, and no certified image flipped by a 20-step PGD attack. The upstream README's "64 tests" is stale by the two photometric tests added with contrast augmentation - counted from the live test files, 62 test functions with two parametrized over three cases each.

Case study: [visionlab.md](visionlab.md)

### LoRALab: width is not predictive rank

[Exact inspector](https://midya8.github.io/loralab/rank.html) - [Source and reproduction](https://github.com/midya8/loralab) - [Proof, schema and reproduction](https://github.com/midya8/loralab/blob/main/PREDICTIVE_RANK.md)

LoRALab's exact-rational inspector separates four numbers that factor width collapses into one: the supplied width, the rank of the merged update matrix, the least rank of an all-input softmax-equivalent update, and the finite-probe rank. At every nonzero epoch, widths 4 and 8 have exact product rank 3 and class-difference rank 2 — the three output rows cap the product rank at three, and the all-input minimum is the rank of the class-row differences, proved above by constructive factors and below by a nonzero minor — while widths 1 and 2 retain ranks 1 and 2. The finite-probe minimum is rank(D X^T), with an attaining extension, an explicit unseen-coordinate counterexample when it differs from the global minimum, and a nonzero probe-nullspace direction whenever the probes fail to span feature space; even a canonical extension that happens to equal D cannot make non-spanning probes complete. Fewer algebraic scalar slots do not prove lower rational storage, runtime memory, inference speed or retained accuracy — the original training still used 524/1048 parameters at widths 4/8.

39 Python tests (all 31 earlier tests unchanged) and seven substantive Node/Chrome tests pin the machinery: 729 ternary 3x2 matrices at all three references against independent Leibniz minors, 11 exact controls, all 124 complete native/browser report hashes, altered factor/minor leaves, every real UI archive selector, 36 layouts, 1,322 selectable exact factor cells, trusted edit invalidation, three downloads and native verification replay. An independent native Bareiss verifier never calls the rank producer, and browser BigInt decodes the original binary64 factors as exact represented ratios. Exact arithmetic certifies a real probability function only — rounded probabilities, argmax, loss, future factor training and hidden layers are outside scope, and exact rank is not tolerance-based numerical rank. Standard softmax-equivalence and rank facts are attributed to Yang et al., LoRA and Higham, not presented as a new algorithm.

Case study: [loralab.md](loralab.md)

### STNLab: a tight bound is not an attained bound

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/stnlab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/stnlab) - [Method and source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/stnlab/blob/main/METHOD.md)

STNLab inspects every implied time difference in a tiny temporal network and refuses to let a tight path stand in for a reachable deadline: an edge [u,v,w] means t[v] - t[u] <= w, a tight path gives a bound, and the bound is only evidence once a complete schedule meets it, with every time point assigned — while an unbounded pair is settled by an anchored ray, not by the absence of a path. A negative cycle shows exactly why no schedule exists, even away from the anchor, and removing one edge returns an alternate implication path or a complete separating schedule. Eleven editable controls include duplicate edges, zero cycles, free components and a chain with bound 100; the anchor t0=0 only removes a common translation, negative times are allowed, and finite bounds are individually attainable, not necessarily jointly selectable.

The insertion stage takes one proposed constraint and returns an exact consistency threshold plus every changed implied bound, with 41 integer weights as separate alternatives and ten new synthetic controls — exact-boundary feasibility, a missing return path, a pre-inconsistent base, duplicate/self-loop cases, a six-point threshold outside the input window and a 12-edge base plus candidate — covered by 17 Python tests and six Node/real-Chrome tests. The projection stage hides time points without losing the bounds their paths imply, lifting jointly feasible retained assignments into full original schedules and exposing retained assignments that naive edge deletion wrongly permits, audited by independent Fourier-Motzkin elimination across 12 projection controls. The conflicts stage enumerates every inclusion-minimal temporal conflict and every inclusion-minimal correction set, with complete hitting-set duality and minimal explicitly distinct from minimum-cardinality. Validation covers all four families: 31 native tests on Python 3.10-3.13, 12 Node/Chrome tests, 316 cross-engine reports, exhaustive tiny networks and installed-wheel reproduction, with Chrome visiting every control proof at 320/390/1280. Six points and twelve constraints, real-valued times, Classic temporal-network theory — not a new optimization algorithm, calendar executor or uncertainty model.

Case study: [stnlab.md](stnlab.md)

### SpecLab: stopping is not censoring

[Live exact stopping inspector](https://midya8.github.io/speclab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/speclab) - [Derivation and six primary sources](https://github.com/midya8/speclab/blob/main/STOPPING.md)

SpecLab's v0.4 extension makes stopping decisions **while drafting**, rather than choosing a complete block length before the round: the safe before-next max(q) rule and the sampled-token keep-and-verify rule preserve the full target law, while the deliberately wrong sample-censoring control gives (5/8,3/8) instead of the target (1/2,1/2), with complete two-position sequence TV 1/8. Keeping a low-confidence stop token preserves the law; censoring it and forgetting it breaks the law — stopping is not censoring. Target-only and every fixed cap 1-3 are full-strength baselines, and unused before-next distribution lookups are charged.

Every stopped leaf, acceptance/residual/bonus branch and joint sequence/count outcome is retained, and a separate verifier enumerates full maximum-length paths, marginalizes unused suffixes and expands decoding states forward. The browser recomputes complete edited conditional tables in BigInt, invalidates stale evidence and downloads reports for independent replay. 61 Python tests retain all 47 earlier tests, with 14 new tests covering 125 binary distribution/threshold controls, seeded prefix-dependent tables, every scalar-leaf tamper and full count conservation; four Node/Chrome tests compare full reports and exercise 12 controls, 604 stopped leaves, 1,503 verification branches, 1,950 exact sequence cells, 36 layouts, trusted edits and actual downloaded evidence. Python 3.10-3.13 and isolated installed-wheel checks retain the original greedy, stochastic and oracle gates. The scope is supplied exact finite tables only — 1-3 tokens, horizon 1-4, max draft length 1-3 — with no EOS, learned predictor, entropy approximation, library default claim, measured latency or new-algorithm claim; stopping changes operation counts, not automatically hardware speed. The negative control is defined here, not attributed to a production library.

Case study: [speclab.md](speclab.md)

### StructGate: the future has a weight

[Live DFA conditioning inspector](https://midya8.github.io/structgate/automaton.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/structgate) - [Exact contract and credited sources](https://github.com/midya8/structgate/blob/main/AUTOMATON.md)

StructGate's v0.3 extension stops asking only which next symbol is legal and asks what future each legal symbol still has: for the default word 01, the source gives it probability 1/20, the viability mask 1/2, and exact global conditioning 1/10. Local masking renormalizes the legal set to p/Z, but exact conditioning weights each next symbol by h(next)/h(prefix), the source probability that a future suffix completes an accepted word, so a conditioned path never walks into a dead end it cannot leave. Stranded mass stays in a failure sink that full total variation must include, and a source-zero conditioning event is explicitly undefined rather than an all-zero distribution. A legal next symbol can still have an unlikely future, and the inspector shows every future mass, transition, selected-word prefix and forward marginal instead of letting the mask hide it.

64 Python tests retain all 54 previous tests, with six Node/actual-Chrome tests retaining all three previous; independent complete-word checks cover 60 seeded small DFAs and all source, local and global marginals, with native Fraction backward recursion and independent BigInt full forward-kernel propagation agreeing on 24 complete reports, 2,043 future rows, 6,550 symbol rows and 2,239 marginal rows. Chrome checks all 156 selected-word prefixes, 24 responsive views and two natively replayed trusted downloads, and an isolated eleven-module install reproduces all new reports while the original nine-module wheel, demo, evaluation and gates remain intact. The scope is at most four symbols, eight DFA states and 32 emitted symbols, with zero-probability conditioning undefined and source-zero branches keeping their local failure mass; the 32-symbol ending control represents 4,294,967,296 possible source words with 192 transition rows, a structural count rather than a speed, RAM or scaling benchmark. Standard finite-state/Doob conditioning, not a new algorithm, random sampler, arbitrary JSON/CFG solver or LLM-quality result, and aggregated marginals are not full-word total variation.

Case study: [structgate.md](structgate.md)

### EvalForge: the raw tail is not the family tail

[Live family inspector](https://midya8.github.io/evalforge/family.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/evalforge) - [Exact contract and sources](https://github.com/midya8/evalforge/blob/main/FAMILY.md)

EvalForge's v0.2 audit asks what changes when the tasks in a comparison aren't independent — they arrive in clusters, and each supplied cluster gets one sign shared across every candidate. The default candidate's raw absolute-sum tail is 1/4, but the family-maximum tail is 1/2: same numbers, a different flip unit, and the evidence looks half as strong once the family is the unit. Repeated-row controls show how the declared flip unit changes the orbit without changing the observed task-weighted sums, and the original regression gate, paired inspector and McNemar calculation stay unchanged underneath. The paired inspector it builds on catches a quieter mistake — a whole-run mean can fall while every matched task improves, because the candidate added hard tasks and dropped easy ones — so EvalForge validates result rows, rejects duplicate ids and recomputes all-task and matched-task summaries separately instead of trusting stored summaries.

102 Python tests retain all 92 prior tests, with seven Node/actual-Chrome tests retaining all three prior tests. Independent original-task relabeling checks 150 seeded matrices and all 729 small two-column controls, and Python block aggregation/bit masks and independent browser Cartesian signs agree on 24 complete reports, 118 task rows, 73 cluster rows, 506 full assignments and 10,474 candidate-by-cluster contributions. Chrome checks all displayed rows, bar geometry, stale/invalid state, both exports and 320/390/1280px containment, and isolated installed API, module and console runs reproduce every report plus all original wheel/adapter/export checks. The scope is at most four candidates, 12 common tasks and eight clusters with integer score ticks 0..1000; zero blocks and ties retain full multiplicity and empty evidence stays undefined. Supplied labels do not prove independent clusters, joint symmetry or randomization, and the complete-joint-null orbit arithmetic is not universal strong-FWER protection under arbitrary partial nulls, a superiority result or a new statistical method.

### MoeLab: a bound is not an attained loss

[Gate regret inspector](https://midya8.github.io/moelab/gate.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/moelab) - [Objective, row proof, complete schema, sources and limits](https://github.com/midya8/moelab/blob/main/GATE-REGRET.md)

MoeLab's gate-regret extension freezes the canonical nominal dispatch, declares probability bounds around every row, then finds one admissible table that maximizes the challenger's retained-mass advantage. The tempting comparison — challenger-best against reference-worst — gives 1/5 on the simplex-coupling control, but its coordinates sum to 11/10, so it is not a table at all; the actual one-table regret is 1/10. On a forced-route control every coefficient cancels and regret is zero, even though the separate tables give a loose 1/5 bound. Both separate tables stay in the report, so a bound is never presented as an attained loss. The capacity dispatch underneath still maximizes dispatched tokens first, then retained gate mass, and two routing optima with the same count and mass can have scalar-update MSE 0 and 25 — gate mass is not task quality.

72 Python and 14 Node/Chrome tests retain the previous 53-Python/nine-Node suite. The regret checks add 500 vertex-oracle row problems and 90 random assignment problems, with every row carrying an attaining point and an equal dual upper bound, and a standalone verifier that enumerates row vertices, complete assignments and suffixes without importing the producer. Chrome checks 30 tables, 58 row proofs, 234 states and 30 layouts, plus two real downloads with native replay. Capacity checks cover 225 probability pairs, 1,024 eligibility/capacity cases and all 813 capacity states, and the route evidence holds 32 complete witnesses and 8,176 evaluation positions. The scope is supplied rational tables with at most seven tokens and four experts; these are assumed gate bounds, not calibrated confidence, and retained-mass regret says nothing about task quality.
