### ScratchGPT: a restart is not a fresh start

[Cache workbench](https://midya8.github.io/scratchgpt/cache.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/scratchgpt) - [Continuation replay](https://midya8.github.io/scratchgpt/) - [Rematerialization](https://midya8.github.io/scratchgpt/remat.html)

ScratchGPT's checkpoint saves named float32 parameters, Adam m/v buffers and clock, both PCG64 train/evaluation states, text identity, vocabulary, dimensions, numerical options, scheduled history and runtime metadata — because a restart has to reproduce the next update, not just reload weights. A separate 1,056-parameter synthetic run keeps all 12 updates and all 13 stop boundaries, and complete continuation reaches the same final state at every boundary in the same environment. Resetting train or evaluation RNG, or dropping Adam moments, changes weights at all 11 interior boundaries; resetting only evaluation RNG changes future validation history, not training weights. Stops at zero and 12 stay controls. The browser independently recomputes Adam arithmetic and transformer forwards from the recorded weights — 38,016 optimizer values and 2,592 logits — and does not train on that page.

The optional rematerialization path discards the forward interior graph and rebuilds blocks with the actual upstream VJP, and every one of the shipped checkpoint’s 419,328 parameter gradients is checked for both block groupings without retraining it. The cache audit takes a copied read-only float32 weight snapshot, rebuilds every layer of the entire final window whenever cropping shifts learned absolute positions, and counts eight full controls with 43 chunk steps, 685 cached logits and 3,256 K/V coordinates. The archived timing run is honest about the limit: caching wins at short prompts — 8.287x and 10.722x at lengths 4 and 16 — but at full context it rebuilds every step and loses slightly, 253.230 ms cached versus 251.783 ms full. 158 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 and 13 Node/Chrome tests retain all prior tests, with the original Shakespeare checkpoint preserved; exactness is same-environment, and the new shipped-checkpoint cache test uses a 1e-5 absolute logit bound and 3e-6 softmax total-variation bound.

Case study: [scratchgpt.md](scratchgpt.md)

### CredalLab: winning every duel is not winning the menu

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/credallab/) - [Decision inspector](https://midya8.github.io/credallab/decisions.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/credallab) - [Decision semantics and sources](https://github.com/midya8/credallab/blob/main/DECISIONS.md)

CredalLab's decision layer makes the gap exact with one menu: actions (3,0), (0,3) and (1,1) over two worlds. The third action defends against each pure rival at a different belief, yet no one common belief makes it best against both at once: it remains strict pairwise maximal and interval-undominated, and it is the unique pure-action maximin choice, but its best shared-belief margin is exactly -1/2 at belief (1/2,1/2), and a fixed 50/50 mixture of its rivals has utility 3/2 in both worlds and excludes it by 1/2 everywhere. Winning each duel is a statement about a different belief per rival; winning the menu is a statement about one belief shared across all rivals, and the two do not compose — the exclusion is proved by the mixture, not by any single rival beating it. The same structure sits underneath in the probability layer: three pairwise-disagreement events over three binary variables can each demand probability 1, yet every binary assignment satisfies either zero or two of them, never all three — the minimum common interval relaxation is exactly 1/3, and at 2/3 each the unique grouped distribution is (0, 1/3, 1/3, 1/3). Each assessment looks valid on its own; the joint model is what fails, and the failure is proved by an exact certificate, not by a failed search.

A second control sharpens the point: changing the third action to (3/2,3/2) makes it E-admissible only at the half/half belief, not at an original credal vertex, so an inspector that checked only the original probability vertices would miss the one belief where it is admissible at all — the implementation solves the menu-intersection margin LP with a free-below margin variable instead. The inspector reports E-admissible, strict pairwise maximal, interval-undominated, pure-action maximin and maximax sets, and replays every range, ordered-pair, margin and projected-mixture primal/dual certificate; a standalone native checker validates supplied evidence without loading the solver or generator, and accepts alternative attaining witnesses. Version 0.2 adds 15 tests for 31 total, with 96 complete Python/BigInt decision reports over 75 feasible menus and 56 excluded actions, 824 rejected primitive decision modifications across three controls and 1,420 input-built HiGHS LPs with largest absolute error 2.220446049250313e-16 at tolerance 1e-8; actual Chrome checks 48 responsive views, 129 action selections, 855 certificate selections, 2,784 independent inequality rows, 1,884 variable rows and 16 downloaded reports natively. The v0.1 probability checks still pass — 16 native tests, 120 complete reports (78 feasible, 42 infeasible, 69 with defined positive-mass conditional bounds), 505 rejected primitive certificate mutations and 690 rebuilt HiGHS LPs including 138 Charnes-Cooper conditional LPs. The scope is six worlds/actions and six assessments with separate 20,000 candidate-basis limits, utilities as bounded exact signed rationals on one common state-independent scale, CLI model input capped at 64 KiB and certificate reports at 8 MiB; classical finite probabilistic satisfiability and linear optimization following the strict-only definitions in Nakharutai, Troffaes and Caiado (2019), not a new inference theorem, learned probability model, credal network or production safety system.

Case study: [credallab.md](credallab.md)

### OrderLab: no improving swap, still not the best order

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/orderlab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/orderlab) - [Method and attributed theory](https://github.com/midya8/orderlab/blob/main/METHOD.md)

OrderLab's default makes the gap concrete with three ballots: `0>1>2` at weight 3, `1>2>0` at weight 2 and `2>0>1` at weight 2, with strict majority margins forming the cycle `0>1>2>0` at `3,3,1`. Start at `1>2>0` and the cost is 10; both adjacent swaps make it worse, by 3 and by 1, so strict descent stops on the spot. The unique global optimum is `0>1>2` at cost 8. Picking the cheaper direction for every pair independently gives a lower bound of 7, but that bound is not a transitive order — it cannot be walked to. The gap between the local stop and the global optimum is 2, and the extra cost one total order must pay above the pair bound is 1, which the report calls `cycle_tax`: a name for the number, not a count of cycles and not a claim about which cycle caused it. A local stopping rule is not a proof of optimality, and the inspector shows the whole census so the difference is visible instead of argued.

The evidence is built to be checked three ways at once: Python places the first candidate in each exact subset recurrence, JavaScript independently computes values by placing the last candidate and scores full orders directly from ballots, and a native replay checker enumerates every restricted permutation and sums ballot inversions directly without importing either solver or the report generator. An optional SciPy/HiGHS binary linear-ordering formulation with every triangle constraint checks one optimum cost per non-singleton profile — a numerical reference at tolerance 1e-8, not the exact certificate. 18 Python tests over 76 complete developed/seeded profiles cover 10,427 full orders, 1,542 subsets, 3,883 transitions and 22 strict cycles, with 1,100 changed derived primitives across three controls all rejected; two Node tests compare 76 complete JS/Python reports with native replay, 65 HiGHS solves over 422 triangle constraints show zero observed optimum cost discrepancy, and actual Chrome checks 48 control-width views with 5,091 order selections and 846 subset selections. The scope is one to six candidates and one to sixteen complete strict ballot rows, weights up to 1,000 each and 10,000 in total, cost bounded by 150,000 — classical Kemeny aggregation, with the subset recurrence credited to Betzler et al., Theorem 4 and the local/global distinction to Dwork et al., not a new theorem, a learned ranker or a real election recommendation. Publication is verified at main 08220e519d0ec39fbc635def504be9ec94dc45da, merged after all six feature/PR jobs passed, with all 46 public files and five deployed assets matching checked source and all 35 deployed screenshots matching inspected local pixels. [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/orderlab/pull/1).

Case study: [orderlab.md](orderlab.md)

### FireLab: balance is not an execution

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/firelab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/firelab) - [Sources and prior work](https://github.com/midya8/firelab/blob/main/SOURCES.md)

FireLab's default makes the gap concrete with three tokens and one transition: the net starts at (A,B,R)=(0,0,2) and is asked for (0,1,1), and t0: A+R -> A+B has net effect (0,+1,-1), so counts (1,0,0) balance exactly — yet t0 cannot fire, because there is no A token. Canceling the shared A term in the incidence matrix must not cancel its enabling guard. Two helper transitions turn R into A and A back into R, and the canonical shortest goal sequence t1 -> t0 -> t2, length 3 with one shortest word, reaches the same goal using counts the proposal omitted. Supplying all three counts makes that multiset executable; remove the helpers and the balanced target becomes globally unreachable. The inspector keeps the three questions separate — does this count vector land exactly at the goal, does any legal ordering execute exactly this vector, and does any legal sequence reach the goal with any counts — because a fixed-count failure is not a global impossibility proof, and a balanced count is not an executable plan.

The evidence is checked three ways at once: the Python generator uses BFS/reverse BFS and forward/backward count-lattice dynamic programming, a separate checker imports input validation only and simulates actual token bags with Floyd-Warshall/incoming-shortest recursion and explicit enumeration of legal multiset words, and JavaScript runs a second runtime with all-pairs distances and count-lattice DP — its browser replay regenerates with that same engine, so it is not called an independent checker, and agreement can share semantic mistakes. 18 native tests cover all 14 original controls, with an exhaustive class of 4,704 ordered two-transition/initial-marking/count cases and 350 seeded requests fully replayed; 114 complete Python/JavaScript reports carry 629 marking rows and 806 lattice rows, all 197 noninput primitive forgeries are rejected per engine, and an optional real PM4Py 2.7.23 reference checks 164 requests with 771 enabling states, 1,541 transition results and 2,834 accepted count words. The scope is 2–4 places and at most 4 transitions, arc entries 0–2, each transition strictly conserving the token total — established Petri-net theory, not a new theorem or a real resource protocol. Publication is verified at main 97fb7a1cea00cbdfea79bf7458234120c2550d6c, merged after all six feature/PR jobs passed, with all 32 public files and five deployed assets matching checked source byte for byte; of 32 unmasked deployed captures, 31 match exactly, and the one 390px boundary-control state table differs in 165 pixels inside a header label with maximum channel difference 56/255, both images inspected with no content or layout change. [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/firelab/pull/1).

Case study: [firelab.md](firelab.md)
### CompileLab: a projected model is not a sum of hidden completions

[Live workbench](https://midya8.github.io/compilelab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/compilelab) - [Sources and prior work](https://github.com/midya8/compilelab/blob/main/SOURCES.md)

CompileLab's projection control makes the gap concrete with one OR and one kept variable: for `x0 OR x1`, keep only x0. Assignment 0 has one satisfying hidden completion, assignment 1 has two. Three full models, two projected retained models. With every literal weighted 1/2 the full mass is 3/4 while the retained-only projected mass is 1; the two fiber completion masses are 1/4 and 1/2, while both projected retained masses are 1/2. The fibers disagree and the retained rows agree — and both numbers are right, because they count different things: the full mass counts assignments over the whole universe, the retained mass counts assignments over the kept variables. Every retained row shows all its satisfying evidence-compatible full extensions, their weights, their count and the lexicographically first full witness, and a zero-mass extension still establishes existential truth. A projected model is not a sum of its hidden completions; it is the fact that at least one exists.

The evidence is checked three ways at once: the native generator builds diagrams by Shannon recursion with a unique triple table and graph dynamic programming, a separate checker imports strict input validation only and enumerates CNF truth with bitmasks, groups residual truth vectors and directly enumerates suffix assignments instead of using the producer’s recurrence, and JavaScript runs a separate row-partition implementation with BigInt rational arithmetic whose browser replay regenerates with the same engine. 15 native tests cover all 12 developed controls, with an exhaustive class of 2,048 clause/keep configurations and 180 seeded inputs independently replayed; 102 complete Python/JavaScript reports carry 90 nonterminals and 1,394 trace rows, all 719 primitive noninput forgeries are rejected per engine, and an optional actual dd 0.6.0 reference checks 574 CNFs with 340 full models and 175 projected assignments. The scope is at most five variables, ten clauses and ten signed literals per clause, with rational weights capped at numerator/denominator 1,000 and a permutation atlas of at most 120 orders — ordinary noncomplemented ROBDDs only, no dynamic-order heuristic, general d-DNNF compiler, formal proof or probability calibration. Publication is verified at main 61e066efdc27714844d18cf2950f2d21151b9aab, merged after all eight latest feature/PR jobs passed, with all 30 public files and six deployed assets matching tested source byte for byte and all 64 unmasked local/live captures pixel-identical. [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/compilelab/pull/1).

Case study: [compilelab.md](compilelab.md)
### ChainLab: the question comes before the path

[Prefix inspector](https://midya8.github.io/chainlab/) · [Path workbench](https://midya8.github.io/chainlab/paths.html) · [Source and reproduction](https://github.com/midya8/chainlab)

MAP minimizes whole-path 0/1 loss, not expected wrong positions. The rational control gives different supported MAP, expected-matches and product-marginal paths; its unconstrained state modes form an impossible string. Locked and independent controls then hold state marginals fixed while whole-path entropy changes from ln(2) to 5 ln(2). These are supplied-model decision and dependence examples, not real-state accuracy or calibration results.

The prefix inspector makes the evidence boundary visible: smoothing and Viterbi use the displayed window, while filtering and forecasts use only the appropriate prefix. The original floating trainer, synthetic data, initialization, training-only iid control, generating reference and test scores remain unchanged. Browser edits do not retrain or become new benchmarks.

The suite has 58 Python and 10 Node tests, including actual Chrome for both inspectors. Exact rational reports have independent tiny exhaustive references and a standalone verifier; entropy remains approximate with a disclosed Decimal80 tolerance. Installed replay checks ten modules, original/evidence commands, both path commands and thirteen rational controls outside the checkout. Bounded inputs, rational-work refusals, browser-scaling limits, different tie rules and rounded-model provenance are explicit in the case study.

Case study: [chainlab.md](chainlab.md)
